I mean, sometimes the next day I’ll think, “Okay, take it easy today,” but I’m not sure if that counts as skipping meals or trying to undo it.\n\nI don’t usually go out and exercise extra or anything planned like that. Mostly I just feel bad and tell myself I’ll do better. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're eating very quickly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, racing on purpose, but when I’m in my truck or out in the workshop, I eat it pretty fast.\n\nHonestly, sometimes I barely even taste it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down before somebody notices or catches me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably true. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s my eating, mostly. It’s kind of embarrassing to say out loud. I know that probably doesn’t sound great.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: Um… not every night, but more than I’d like to admit. I know that’s probably important to mention.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m making a choice and I can stop, but honestly, once I start it kind of feels like a switch flips. I’ll even be thinking, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” but my hands just keep reaching for more anyway. It’s… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make a big drama out of it, because I know people have worse problems, but afterward I feel pretty ashamed. I don’t like admitting that, but… yeah, guilt is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating large amounts of food by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’d say I really do anything like that. I’d be embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away.\n\nThat’s probably what you mean, right?\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused problems at work or in your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Not really at work, no… I mean, I’m still showing up and doing my job, and I’m keeping up with church and all that. I’m not trying to shut people out, but I guess that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this way when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down before somebody notices or catches me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably true. I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds like I have no self-control, but once I start, it’s like something flips.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hand’s already reaching for more. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time. I know I should stop… I just don’t, at least not right then. I mean… I’m sure stress doesn’t help anything, but I haven’t really sat down and connected it like, “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.”\n\nSometimes it just kind of happens, or at least that’s how it feels to me. I don’t like admitting that, but… yeah, guilt is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating large amounts of food by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’d say I really do anything like that. I mean, sometimes the next day I’ll think, “Okay, take it easy today,” but I’m not sure if that counts as skipping meals or trying to undo it.\n\nI don’t usually go out and exercise extra or anything planned like that. Mostly I just feel bad and tell myself I’ll do better. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're eating very quickly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, racing on purpose, but when I’m in my truck or out in the workshop, I eat it pretty fast.\n\nHonestly, sometimes I barely even taste it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down before somebody notices or catches me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably true. I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds like I have no self-control, but once I start, it’s like something flips.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hand’s already reaching for more. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time. I know I should stop… I just don’t, at least not right then. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these episodes in secret, away from others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be sneaky in some awful way, but… I’ll act like I’m not that hungry at dinner with my wife, and then later I’ll go out to the truck or wait until she’s busy. I’d be embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away.\n\nThat’s probably what you mean, right?\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused problems at work or in your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Not really at work, no… I mean, I’m still showing up and doing my job, and I’m keeping up with church and all that. So I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart.\n\nAt home, though… yeah, maybe some. I’ve been a little more withdrawn than I should be. I spend a lot of energy trying to explain why I stopped somewhere, or why I’m not hungry at dinner, and I know my wife can tell something’s off. I’m not trying to shut people out, but I guess that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this way when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I’m sure stress doesn’t help anything, but I haven’t really sat down and connected it like, “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.”\n\nSometimes it just kind of happens, or at least that’s how it feels to me. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer and make it sound more clear-cut than it is. Maybe I should pay more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feelings of stress, anxiety, or being upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that, no… not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I’m sure if I’m worn out or frustrated it probably doesn’t help, but I haven’t noticed a direct pattern like, “I’m anxious, so I eat.” Sometimes it feels more like it just sort of starts before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very definite answer. I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the part I don’t like admitting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be reckless, but once I start, it feels like something kind of flips. I’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hands are still reaching in for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it just doesn’t feel as simple as deciding to stop in that moment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

So I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart.\n\nAt home, though… yeah, maybe some. I’ve been a little more withdrawn than I should be. I spend a lot of energy trying to explain why I stopped somewhere, or why I’m not hungry at dinner, and I know my wife can tell something’s off. I’m not trying to shut people out, but I guess that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this way when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I’m sure stress doesn’t help anything, but I haven’t really sat down and connected it like, “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.”\n\nSometimes it just kind of happens, or at least that’s how it feels to me. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer and make it sound more clear-cut than it is. Maybe I should pay more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feelings of stress, anxiety, or being upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that, no… not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I’m sure if I’m worn out or frustrated it probably doesn’t help, but I haven’t noticed a direct pattern like, “I’m anxious, so I eat.” Sometimes it feels more like it just sort of starts before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very definite answer. I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the part I don’t like admitting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be reckless, but once I start, it feels like something kind of flips. I’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hands are still reaching in for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it just doesn’t feel as simple as deciding to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you feel a strong urge to continue eating, or is it more that you just don't want to stop?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the urge, yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I have no responsibility for it, but it doesn’t feel like just, “Oh, I’d rather keep eating.”\n\nOnce I start, it’s like that switch flips, and even while I’m telling myself, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” my hands are still reaching for more. So… yeah, it feels more like I can’t get myself to stop in that moment.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s my eating, mostly. It’s kind of embarrassing to say out loud. After late shifts, I’ll stop and get takeout, and I tell myself it’s just dinner, but sometimes it’s like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and pick through the leftovers in the garage, almost like having a second supper. I know that probably doesn’t sound great.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: Um… not every night, but more than I’d like to admit. For the past few months, I’d say there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens, and if work’s been rough or I’m on late shifts, it can be two or three nights. I know that’s probably important to mention.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m making a choice and I can stop, but honestly, once I start it kind of feels like a switch flips. I’ll even be thinking, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” but my hands just keep reaching for more anyway. Maybe I should pay more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feelings of stress, anxiety, or being upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that, no… not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I’m sure if I’m worn out or frustrated it probably doesn’t help, but I haven’t noticed a direct pattern like, “I’m anxious, so I eat.” Sometimes it feels more like it just sort of starts before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very definite answer. I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the part I don’t like admitting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be reckless, but once I start, it feels like something kind of flips. I’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hands are still reaching in for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it just doesn’t feel as simple as deciding to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you feel a strong urge to continue eating, or is it more that you just don't want to stop?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the urge, yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I have no responsibility for it, but it doesn’t feel like just, “Oh, I’d rather keep eating.”\n\nOnce I start, it’s like that switch flips, and even while I’m telling myself, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” my hands are still reaching for more. So… yeah, it feels more like I can’t get myself to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Pretty often, I guess… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but afterward I usually don’t feel good about myself.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but I feel ashamed, kind of disgusted with myself. I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined, dependable guy, and then after that happens it feels like I’ve been sneaking around or letting myself down.\n\nIs that… is that the kind of thing you mean by guilty?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s my eating, mostly. It’s kind of embarrassing to say out loud. After late shifts, I’ll stop and get takeout, and I tell myself it’s just dinner, but sometimes it’s like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and pick through the leftovers in the garage, almost like having a second supper. I know that probably doesn’t sound great.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: Um… not every night, but more than I’d like to admit. For the past few months, I’d say there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens, and if work’s been rough or I’m on late shifts, it can be two or three nights. I know that’s probably important to mention.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m making a choice and I can stop, but honestly, once I start it kind of feels like a switch flips. I’ll even be thinking, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” but my hands just keep reaching for more anyway. It’s… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make a big drama out of it, because I know people have worse problems, but afterward I feel pretty ashamed. Kind of disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined, dependable guy, and when I’m doing that it feels like I’m sneaking around or letting myself down. I don’t like admitting that, but… yeah, guilt is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating large amounts of food by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’d say I really do anything like that. I mean, sometimes the next day I’ll think, “Okay, take it easy today,” but I’m not sure if that counts as skipping meals or trying to undo it.\n\nI don’t usually go out and exercise extra or anything planned like that. Mostly I just feel bad and tell myself I’ll do better. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're eating very quickly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, racing on purpose, but when I’m in my truck or out in the workshop, I eat it pretty fast.\n\nHonestly, sometimes I barely even taste it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down before somebody notices or catches me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably true. I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds like I have no self-control, but once I start, it’s like something flips.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hand’s already reaching for more. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time. I know I should stop… I just don’t, at least not right then. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these episodes in secret, away from others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be sneaky in some awful way, but… I’ll act like I’m not that hungry at dinner with my wife, and then later I’ll go out to the truck or wait until she’s busy. I’d be embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away.\n\nThat’s probably what you mean, right?\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused problems at work or in your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Not really at work, no… I mean, I’m still showing up and doing my job, and I’m keeping up with church and all that. So I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart.\n\nAt home, though… yeah, maybe some. I’ve been a little more withdrawn than I should be. I spend a lot of energy trying to explain why I stopped somewhere, or why I’m not hungry at dinner, and I know my wife can tell something’s off. I’m not trying to shut people out, but I guess that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this way when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer and make it sound more clear-cut than it is. I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the part I don’t like admitting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be reckless, but once I start, it feels like something kind of flips. I’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hands are still reaching in for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it just doesn’t feel as simple as deciding to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you feel a strong urge to continue eating, or is it more that you just don't want to stop?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the urge, yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I have no responsibility for it, but it doesn’t feel like just, “Oh, I’d rather keep eating.”\n\nOnce I start, it’s like that switch flips, and even while I’m telling myself, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” my hands are still reaching for more. So… yeah, it feels more like I can’t get myself to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Pretty often, I guess… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but afterward I usually don’t feel good about myself.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but I feel ashamed, kind of disgusted with myself. I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined, dependable guy, and then after that happens it feels like I’ve been sneaking around or letting myself down.\n\nIs that… is that the kind of thing you mean by guilty?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after every episode of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say every single time… I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nBut most times, yes. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s my eating, mostly. It’s kind of embarrassing to say out loud. After late shifts, I’ll stop and get takeout, and I tell myself it’s just dinner, but sometimes it’s like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and pick through the leftovers in the garage, almost like having a second supper. I know that probably doesn’t sound great.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: Um… not every night, but more than I’d like to admit. For the past few months, I’d say there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens, and if work’s been rough or I’m on late shifts, it can be two or three nights. I know that’s probably important to mention.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m making a choice and I can stop, but honestly, once I start it kind of feels like a switch flips. I’ll even be thinking, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” but my hands just keep reaching for more anyway. It’s… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make a big drama out of it, because I know people have worse problems, but afterward I feel pretty ashamed. Kind of disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined, dependable guy, and when I’m doing that it feels like I’m sneaking around or letting myself down. I don’t like admitting that, but… yeah, guilt is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating large amounts of food by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’d say I really do anything like that. I mean, sometimes the next day I’ll think, “Okay, take it easy today,” but I’m not sure if that counts as skipping meals or trying to undo it.\n\nI don’t usually go out and exercise extra or anything planned like that. Mostly I just feel bad and tell myself I’ll do better. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're eating very quickly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, racing on purpose, but when I’m in my truck or out in the workshop, I eat it pretty fast.\n\nHonestly, sometimes I barely even taste it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down before somebody notices or catches me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably true. I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds like I have no self-control, but once I start, it’s like something flips.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hand’s already reaching for more. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time. I know I should stop… I just don’t, at least not right then. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these episodes in secret, away from others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be sneaky in some awful way, but… I’ll act like I’m not that hungry at dinner with my wife, and then later I’ll go out to the truck or wait until she’s busy. I’d be embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away.\n\nThat’s probably what you mean, right?\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused problems at work or in your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Not really at work, no… I mean, I’m still showing up and doing my job, and I’m keeping up with church and all that. So I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart.\n\nAt home, though… yeah, maybe some. I’ve been a little more withdrawn than I should be. I spend a lot of energy trying to explain why I stopped somewhere, or why I’m not hungry at dinner, and I know my wife can tell something’s off. I’m not trying to shut people out, but I guess that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this way when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I’m sure stress doesn’t help anything, but I haven’t really sat down and connected it like, “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.”\n\nSometimes it just kind of happens, or at least that’s how it feels to me. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer and make it sound more clear-cut than it is. Maybe I should pay more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feelings of stress, anxiety, or being upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that, no… not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I’m sure if I’m worn out or frustrated it probably doesn’t help, but I haven’t noticed a direct pattern like, “I’m anxious, so I eat.” Sometimes it feels more like it just sort of starts before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very definite answer. Maybe I should pay more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feelings of stress, anxiety, or being upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that, no… not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I’m sure if I’m worn out or frustrated it probably doesn’t help, but I haven’t noticed a direct pattern like, “I’m anxious, so I eat.” Sometimes it feels more like it just sort of starts before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very definite answer. I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the part I don’t like admitting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be reckless, but once I start, it feels like something kind of flips. I’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hands are still reaching in for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it just doesn’t feel as simple as deciding to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you feel a strong urge to continue eating, or is it more that you just don't want to stop?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the urge, yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I have no responsibility for it, but it doesn’t feel like just, “Oh, I’d rather keep eating.”\n\nOnce I start, it’s like that switch flips, and even while I’m telling myself, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” my hands are still reaching for more. So… yeah, it feels more like I can’t get myself to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Pretty often, I guess… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but afterward I usually don’t feel good about myself.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but I feel ashamed, kind of disgusted with myself. I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined, dependable guy, and then after that happens it feels like I’ve been sneaking around or letting myself down.\n\nIs that… is that the kind of thing you mean by guilty?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after every episode of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say every single time… I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nBut most times, yes. Afterward I feel ashamed, and kind of disgusted with myself. I’ve always tried to be a disciplined, dependable guy, so when it happens, it feels like I’m sneaking around or letting myself down.\n\nThat’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after almost every time you eat a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every time, I’d say. I don’t love admitting that, but afterward I usually feel ashamed, and kind of disgusted with myself.\n\nIt’s like… I’ve always seen myself as a disciplined, dependable person, and when this happens it feels like I’m sneaking around or letting myself down. So yes, I think “almost every time” is fair.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess it’s my eating, mostly. It’s kind of embarrassing to say out loud. After late shifts, I’ll stop and get takeout, and I tell myself it’s just dinner, but sometimes it’s like two burgers, fries, a shake… and then I still go home and pick through the leftovers in the garage, almost like having a second supper. I know that probably doesn’t sound great.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating large amounts of food happen?\n\nPatient: Um… not every night, but more than I’d like to admit. For the past few months, I’d say there’s usually at least one night a week where it happens, and if work’s been rough or I’m on late shifts, it can be two or three nights. I know that’s probably important to mention.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m making a choice and I can stop, but honestly, once I start it kind of feels like a switch flips. I’ll even be thinking, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” but my hands just keep reaching for more anyway. It’s… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make a big drama out of it, because I know people have worse problems, but afterward I feel pretty ashamed. Kind of disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined, dependable guy, and when I’m doing that it feels like I’m sneaking around or letting myself down. I don’t like admitting that, but… yeah, guilt is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating large amounts of food by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’d say I really do anything like that. I mean, sometimes the next day I’ll think, “Okay, take it easy today,” but I’m not sure if that counts as skipping meals or trying to undo it.\n\nI don’t usually go out and exercise extra or anything planned like that. Mostly I just feel bad and tell myself I’ll do better. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're eating very quickly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, racing on purpose, but when I’m in my truck or out in the workshop, I eat it pretty fast.\n\nHonestly, sometimes I barely even taste it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down before somebody notices or catches me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably true. I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds like I have no self-control, but once I start, it’s like something flips.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hand’s already reaching for more. I spend a lot of energy trying to explain why I stopped somewhere, or why I’m not hungry at dinner, and I know my wife can tell something’s off. I’m not trying to shut people out, but I guess that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this way when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I’m sure stress doesn’t help anything, but I haven’t really sat down and connected it like, “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.”\n\nSometimes it just kind of happens, or at least that’s how it feels to me. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer and make it sound more clear-cut than it is. Maybe I should pay more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feelings of stress, anxiety, or being upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that, no… not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I’m sure if I’m worn out or frustrated it probably doesn’t help, but I haven’t noticed a direct pattern like, “I’m anxious, so I eat.” Sometimes it feels more like it just sort of starts before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very definite answer. I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the part I don’t like admitting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be reckless, but once I start, it feels like something kind of flips. I’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hands are still reaching in for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it just doesn’t feel as simple as deciding to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you feel a strong urge to continue eating, or is it more that you just don't want to stop?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the urge, yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I have no responsibility for it, but it doesn’t feel like just, “Oh, I’d rather keep eating.”\n\nOnce I start, it’s like that switch flips, and even while I’m telling myself, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” my hands are still reaching for more. So… yeah, it feels more like I can’t get myself to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Pretty often, I guess… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but afterward I usually don’t feel good about myself.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but I feel ashamed, kind of disgusted with myself. I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined, dependable guy, and then after that happens it feels like I’ve been sneaking around or letting myself down.\n\nIs that… is that the kind of thing you mean by guilty?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after every episode of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say every single time… I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nBut most times, yes. I know that’s probably important to mention.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of how much you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’d like to say I’m making a choice and I can stop, but honestly, once I start it kind of feels like a switch flips. I’ll even be thinking, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” but my hands just keep reaching for more anyway. It’s… not a great feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty or ashamed after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make a big drama out of it, because I know people have worse problems, but afterward I feel pretty ashamed. Kind of disgusted with myself, honestly.\n\nI’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined, dependable guy, and when I’m doing that it feels like I’m sneaking around or letting myself down. I don’t like admitting that, but… yeah, guilt is definitely there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to make up for these episodes of eating large amounts of food by doing something to counteract the calories, like exercising more or skipping meals the next day?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know that I’d say I really do anything like that. I mean, sometimes the next day I’ll think, “Okay, take it easy today,” but I’m not sure if that counts as skipping meals or trying to undo it.\n\nI don’t usually go out and exercise extra or anything planned like that. Mostly I just feel bad and tell myself I’ll do better. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During these episodes, do you feel like you're eating very quickly?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I wouldn’t say I’m, you know, racing on purpose, but when I’m in my truck or out in the workshop, I eat it pretty fast.\n\nHonestly, sometimes I barely even taste it. It’s more like I’m just trying to get it down before somebody notices or catches me, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating once you've started during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably true. I don’t like saying it that way, because it sounds like I have no self-control, but once I start, it’s like something flips.\n\nI’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hand’s already reaching for more. It’s not like I’m enjoying it the whole time. I know I should stop… I just don’t, at least not right then. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat during these episodes in secret, away from others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be sneaky in some awful way, but… I’ll act like I’m not that hungry at dinner with my wife, and then later I’ll go out to the truck or wait until she’s busy. I’d be embarrassed for her to see how much I’m actually putting away.\n\nThat’s probably what you mean, right?\n\nDoctor: Has your eating behavior caused problems at work or in your relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Not really at work, no… I mean, I’m still showing up and doing my job, and I’m keeping up with church and all that. So I don’t want to make it sound like everything’s falling apart.\n\nAt home, though… yeah, maybe some. I’ve been a little more withdrawn than I should be. I spend a lot of energy trying to explain why I stopped somewhere, or why I’m not hungry at dinner, and I know my wife can tell something’s off. I’m not trying to shut people out, but I guess that’s kind of what happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat this way when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I’m sure stress doesn’t help anything, but I haven’t really sat down and connected it like, “I’m upset, so now I’m going to eat.”\n\nSometimes it just kind of happens, or at least that’s how it feels to me. I don’t want to give you the wrong answer and make it sound more clear-cut than it is. Maybe I should pay more attention to that?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by feelings of stress, anxiety, or being upset?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I can honestly say that, no… not in a clear way.\n\nI mean, I’m sure if I’m worn out or frustrated it probably doesn’t help, but I haven’t noticed a direct pattern like, “I’m anxious, so I eat.” Sometimes it feels more like it just sort of starts before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nI’m sorry, I know that’s not a very definite answer. I just don’t want to say yes if I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the part I don’t like admitting.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be reckless, but once I start, it feels like something kind of flips. I’ll be telling myself, “Roy, just shut the bag and go back inside,” and at the same time my hands are still reaching in for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad. I’m not trying to make excuses—it just doesn’t feel as simple as deciding to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control your eating during these episodes because you feel a strong urge to continue eating, or is it more that you just don't want to stop?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the urge, yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I have no responsibility for it, but it doesn’t feel like just, “Oh, I’d rather keep eating.”\n\nOnce I start, it’s like that switch flips, and even while I’m telling myself, “Roy, shut the bag and go inside,” my hands are still reaching for more. So… yeah, it feels more like I can’t get myself to stop in that moment.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel guilty after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Pretty often, I guess… I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but afterward I usually don’t feel good about myself.\n\nIt’s embarrassing to say, but I feel ashamed, kind of disgusted with myself. I’ve always thought of myself as a disciplined, dependable guy, and then after that happens it feels like I’ve been sneaking around or letting myself down.\n\nIs that… is that the kind of thing you mean by guilty?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after every episode of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say every single time… I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is.\n\nBut most times, yes. Afterward I feel ashamed, and kind of disgusted with myself. I’ve always tried to be a disciplined, dependable guy, so when it happens, it feels like I’m sneaking around or letting myself down.\n\nThat’s probably the honest answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after almost every time you eat a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yes… almost every time, I’d say. I don’t love admitting that, but afterward I usually feel ashamed, and kind of disgusted with myself.\n\nIt’s like… I’ve always seen myself as a disciplined, dependable person, and when this happens it feels like I’m sneaking around or letting myself down. So yes, I think “almost every time” is fair.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after almost every episode of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to say yes. Almost every time.\n\nI might try to brush it off at first, but afterward I usually feel ashamed, and kind of disgusted with myself. I’ve always thought of myself as disciplined and dependable, so when this happens it feels like I’m sneaking around or letting myself down. That’s probably the best way I can put it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after almost every episode of eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yes… I know I’ve said it a couple ways, but yes, almost every time.\n\nI don’t feel good about it afterward. I feel ashamed, and kind of disgusted with myself. I try to be a disciplined, dependable person, so when I do that it feels like I’m hiding something or sneaking around… like I’ve let myself down."
